Local Well-posedness and Incompressible Limit of the Free-Boundary Problem in Compressible Elastodynamics
Junyan Zhang

Abstract
We consider 3D free-boundary compressible elastodynamic system under the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition. It describes the motion of an isentropic inviscid elastic medium with moving boundary. The deformation tensor is assumed to satisfy the neo-Hookean linear elasticity. The local well-posedness was proved by Trakhinin [ 85 ] by Nash-Moser iteration. In this paper, we give a new proof of the local well-posedness by the combination of classical energy method and hyperbolic approach. In the proof, we apply the tangential smoothing method to define the approximation system. The key observation is that the structure of the wave equation of pressure together with Christodoulou-Lindblad [ 14 ] elliptic estimates reduces the energy estimates to the control of tangentially-differentiated wave equations despite a potential loss of derivative in the source term. To the best of our knowledge, we first establish the nonlinear energy estimate without loss of regularity for free-boundary compressible elastodynamics. The energy estimate is also uniform in sound speed which yields the incompressible limit, i.e., the solutions of the free-boundary compressible elastodynamic equations converge to the incompressible counterpart provided the convergence of initial datum. It is worth emphasizing that our method is completely applicable to compressible Euler equations. Our observation also shows that it is not necessary to include the full time derivatives in the boundary energy and analyze higher order wave equations as in Lindblad-Luo [ 60 ] and Luo [ 62 ] even if we require the energy is uniform in sound speed. Moreover, the enhanced regularity for compressible Euler equations obtained in [ 60 , 62 ] can still be recovered for a slightly compressible elastic medium by further delicate analysis of the Alinhac good unknowns, which is completely different from Euler equations.



Mathematics Subject Classification (2020):
35L65, 35Q35, 74B10, 76N10, 76N30.

Keywords.
Compressible fluids, Neo-Hookean elastodynamics, Inviscid flows, Free boundary problem, Well-posedness, Incompressible limit.

1
Introduction

We consider 3D compressible elastodynamic equations describing the motion of an isentropic inviscid elastic medium with free boundary
[
21
,
32
]

\begin{cases}~\rho D_{t}u=-\nabla p+\text{div }(\rho\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{\top})~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D},\\
D_{t}\rho+\rho\text{div }u=0~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D},\\
D_{t}\mathbf{F}=\nabla u\mathbf{F}~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(1.1)

Here
\mathcal{D}:=\bigcup_{0\leq t\leq T}\{t\}\times\mathcal{D}_{t}
and
\mathcal{D}_{t}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the domain occupied by the elastic medium at time
t
.
\nabla:=(\partial_{x_{1}},\partial_{x_{2}},\partial_{x_{3}})
is the standard spatial derivative and
\text{div }X:=\partial_{x_{i}}X^{i}
is the standard divergence for any vector field
X
.
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla
is the material derivative. Throughout this paper,
X^{i}=\delta^{li}X_{l}
for any vector field
X
, i.e., we use Einstein summation convention. The quantities
u,\rho,p
denotes the velocity, density and fluid pressure of the elastic medium.
\mathbf{F}:=(\mathbf{F}_{ij})_{3\times 3}
is the deformation tensor and
\mathbf{F}^{\top}
denotes its transpose matrix.
\rho\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{\top}
is the Cauchy-Green stress tensor in the case of compressible neo-Hookean linear elasticity.
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+u\cdot\nabla
is the material derivative.

We impose the equation of state as

p=p(\rho)\text{ being a strictly increasing function of }\rho,\text{ for }\rho\geq\bar{\rho_{0}}

(1.2)

where
\bar{\rho_{0}}:=\rho|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}
is a positive constant and we set
\bar{\rho_{0}}=1
throughout this manuscript, which means the elastic medium is isentropic with strictly positive density. We also impose the divergence constraints on the deformation tensor

\text{div }(\rho\mathbf{F}^{\top})=0.

(1.3)

This will not make the system be over-determined because we only require it holds for the initial data and it automatically propagates to any time (cf. Trakhinin
[
85
, Proposition 2.1]
). Note that the system (
1.1
) together with (
1.2
) and (
1.3
) describes the motion of elastic waves in a compressible inviscid neo-Hookean linear material corresponding to the elastic energy
W(\mathbf{F})=\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{F}|^{2}
. It also arises as the inviscid limit of the compressible visco-elastodynamics
[
21
,
32
]
of the Oldroyd type
[
70
,
71
]
.

Next we introduce the boundary conditions

\begin{cases}V(\partial\mathcal{D}_{t})=u\cdot n~~~&\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D},\\
p=0~~~&\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D},\\
\mathbf{F}^{\top}\cdot n=\mathbf{0}~~~&\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(1.4)

The first boundary condition shows that the boundary moves with the velocity of the fluid. It can be considered as the definition of free-boundary problem, and can be equivalently written as
D_{t}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}\in\mathcal{T}(\partial\mathcal{D})
where
\mathcal{T}(\partial\mathcal{D})
denotes the tangential bundle of

\partial\mathcal{D}
, or
(1,u)
is tangent to
\partial\mathcal{D}
. The second condition
p=0
means that outside the fluid region is the vacuum. The third condition means that the deformation tensor is tangential on the boundary, which is also required for initial data only and then automatically propagates. Here
n
denotes the exterior unit normal vector to
\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}
.

Remark
.

The boundary condition
\mathbf{F}^{\top}\cdot n=\mathbf{0}
originates from the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions for the vortex sheets in compressible elastodynamics
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
]
. It leads to
\det\mathbf{F}=0
on the boundary. Such “degeneracy” is due to the mathematical formulation of the singular vortex sheet structure in elastic fluids. The same “degenaracy” takes place for the free-boundary problems and vortex sheets problems for both incompressible
[
34
,
30
,
51
,
52
,
94
]
and compressible elastodynamics
[
85
]
. For more illustrations, we refer readers to
[
9
, Remark 2.1]
.

Energy conservation law

Throughout this paper, we use Einstein summation convention, i.e., repeated indices is summation on this index. We define
Q(\rho):=\int_{1}^{\rho}p(R)/R^{2}~dR
. Under (
1.4
), the system (
1.1
) has the following energy conservation law

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho|u|^{2}+\rho|\mathbf{F}|^{2}\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho Q(\rho)\,dx\right)

(1.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho u^{i}\cdot D_{t}u_{i}\,dx+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho\mathbf{F}_{ij}D_{t}\mathbf{F}_{ij}\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\frac{p(\rho)}{\rho}D_{t}\rho\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}(u\cdot\nabla)p\,dx+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho u^{i}\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}_{ij}\,dx+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho\mathbf{F}_{ij}\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}u^{i}\,dx+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}p(-\text{div }u)\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}u^{i}n_{i}p\,dS+\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}p\text{div }u\,dx-\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}p\text{div }u\,dx

\displaystyle-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}u^{i}\mathbf{F}_{ij}\,dx-\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{\Omega}u^{i}\underbrace{\partial_{k}(\rho\mathbf{F}_{kj})}_{=0}\mathbf{F}_{ij}\,dx+\int_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho u^{i}\underbrace{\mathbf{F}_{kj}n_{k}}_{=0}\mathbf{F}_{ij}\,dS+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\int_{\mathcal{D}_{t}}\rho\mathbf{F}_{ij}\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}u^{i}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Enthalpy formulation

Before further introducing the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition and more physical constraints, we could simplify the system by introducing the enthalpy to replace pressure and density. Define
\mathbf{F}_{j}:=(\mathbf{F}_{1j},\mathbf{F}_{2j},\mathbf{F}_{3j})
to be the
j
-th column of
\mathbf{F}
. Then we have

0=\text{div }(\rho\mathbf{F}^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}(\rho\mathbf{F}_{kj})\Rightarrow\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}_{kj}=-\mathbf{F}_{kj}\frac{\partial_{k}\rho}{\rho}=-\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}\ln\rho=:-(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)\ln\rho,

and thus

\text{div }(\rho\mathbf{F}\mathbf{F}^{\top})_{i}:=\partial_{k}(\rho\mathbf{F}_{ij}\mathbf{F}_{kj})=\underbrace{\partial_{k}(\rho\mathbf{F}_{kj})}_{=0}\mathbf{F}_{ij}+\rho\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}_{ij}=(\rho(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{F}_{j})_{i}.

The second equation reads
\text{div }u=-D_{t}(\ln\rho)
and the third equation reads

D_{t}\mathbf{F}_{ij}=(\nabla u\mathbf{F})_{ij}:=\mathbf{F}_{kj}\partial_{k}u_{i}=(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)u_{i}.

The boundary condition on
\mathbf{F}
is expressed as
0=(\mathbf{F}^{\top}\cdot n)_{j}=\mathbf{F}_{ij}n_{i}=:\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot n
.

Now we introduce the “enthalpy”
\mathfrak{h}
as an increasing function of
\rho
defined by
\mathfrak{h}(\rho):=\int_{1}^{\rho}\frac{p^{\prime}(r)}{r}~dr
and
e(\mathfrak{h}):=\ln\rho(\mathfrak{h})
. Under the enthalpy formulation,
\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{i}p=\partial_{i}\mathfrak{h}
. Then the system (
1.1
) can be re-written as

\begin{cases}D_{t}u=-\nabla\mathfrak{h}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{F}_{j}~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D},\\
\text{div }u=-D_{t}e(\mathfrak{h})=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h})D_{t}\mathfrak{h}~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D},\\
D_{t}\mathbf{F}_{j}=(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)u~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D},\\
(\text{div }\mathbf{F}^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}_{kj}=-(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)e(\mathfrak{h})=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h})(\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot\nabla)\mathfrak{h}~~~&\text{in }\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(1.6)

together with the boundary condition

\begin{cases}D_{t}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}}\in\mathcal{T}(\partial\mathcal{D})~~~&\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D},\\
\mathfrak{h}=0~~~&\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D},\\
\mathbf{F}_{j}\cdot n=0~~~&\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D}.\end{cases}

(1.7)

The new system looks quite similar to the incompressible elastodynamic equations, where
\mathfrak{h}
plays the role as
p
of the incompressible counterpart, while
\text{div }u
and
\text{div }\mathbf{F}^{\top}
is no longer zero yet determined as a function of
\rho
and thus of
\mathfrak{h}
. In order for the initial-boundary value problem of (
1.6
)-(
1.7
) being solvable, we need to impose some other natural physical conditions and initial data satisfying the compatibility conditions.

Physical constraints

First we impose the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition on the free surface

-\frac{\partial\mathfrak{h}}{\partial n}\geq c_{0}>0~~~\text{ on }\partial\mathcal{D}_{t},

(1.8)

where
c_{0}
is a given constant. This is equivalent to the natural physical condition
-\frac{\partial p}{\partial n}\geq c_{0}^{\prime}>0
in the study of the motion of a free-boundary fluid which says that the pressure is larger in the interior than on the boundary. Ebin
[
26
]
proved the ill-posedness of incompressible Euler equations when (
1.8
) is violated. The condition (
1.8
) is only required for the initial data: We will justify this condition by proving
-\frac{\partial\mathfrak{h}}{\partial n}
on the boundary is a
C_{t,x}^{0,1/4}
function, and thus (
1.8
) propagates in a positive time interval.

Next we impose the following natural conditions on
e(\mathfrak{h})
: For each fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, there exists a constant
A>1
such that

|e^{(k)}(\mathfrak{h})|\leq A\text{ and }|e^{(k)}(\mathfrak{h})|\leq A|e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h})|^{k}\leq A|e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h})|~~\forall k\leq m+1.

(1.9)

Compatibility conditions on initial data

Finally we require the initial data
(u_{0},\mathbf{F}^{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})
to satisfy the compatibility conditions at the boundary. From (
1.6
)-(
1.7
) we know
\text{div }u|_{\{0\}\times\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=0
and
\mathfrak{h}_{0}|_{\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=0
is needed, which is called the 0-th order compatibility condition. We define the
m
-th order compatibility conditions to be

D_{t}^{k}\mathfrak{h}|_{\{0\}\times\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=0,~~~k=0,1,\cdots,m.

(1.10)

We will prove in Section
8
that such initial data must exist provided that the sound speed
c^{2}:=p^{\prime}(\rho)
(or equivalently
1/e^{\prime}(h)
) is suitably large.

Given initial data
(u_{0},\mathbf{F}^{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0},\mathcal{D}_{0})
satisfying the compatibility conditions and constraints (
1.3
),
{\mathbf{F}}^{\top}\cdot n|_{\{0\}\times\partial\mathcal{D}_{0}}=\mathbf{0}
as well as Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition (
1.8
) at
t=0
, where
\mathcal{D}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a simply-connected bounded domain, we want to find a set
\mathcal{D}_{t}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
and velocity
u
, enthalpy
\mathfrak{h}
and deformation tensor
\mathbf{F}
solving the system (
1.6
)-(
1.10
). In this manuscript, we aim to prove the local well-posedness of (
1.6
)-(
1.10
) with energy estimates and the incompressible limit.

1.1
History and Background

The study of both free-surface fluid and elastodynamics has a long history and has blossomed in the recent decades. Let us first review the results on the free-boundary Euler equations. For the incompressible case, Wu
[
88
,
89
]
on the local well-posedness (LWP) of full water wave system have been considered as the first breakthrough in the study of free-surface perfect fluid. We also refer to Lannes
[
45
,
5
,
66
]
for the study of local theory of incompressible irrotational water wave. When the vorticity is nonzero, Christodoulou-Lindblad
[
14
]
first established the a priori estimates without loss of regularity. Lindblad
[
56
,
58
]
proved the local well-posedness by using Nash-Moser iteration. Coutand-Shkoller
[
17
,
18
]
introduced the tangential smoothing method to proved the local well-posedness with or without surface tension and avoid the loss of regularity. See also
[
93
,
3
]
for the case without surface tension and
[
75
,
79
,
80
,
81
]
for the nonzero surface tension case.

The study for a free-surface compressible fluid is much more difficult because the pressure is governed by a wave equation instead of being a Lagrangian multiplier in the incompressible case. We list the results in the case of a liquid. Lindblad
[
57
,
59
]
proved the LWP by Nash-Moser iteration and then Trakhinin
[
84
]
extended the LWP to non-isentropic, non-relativistic and relativistic liquid with gravity in an unbounded domain via hyperbolic approach and also Nash-Moser iteration. The first a priori estimates without loss of regularity was established by Lindblad-Luo
[
60
]
and then was extended to a compressible water wave with vorticity Luo
[
62
]
. Ginsberg-Lindblad-Luo
[
28
]
proved the LWP for the self-gravitating liquid. Then the author joint with Luo
[
63
]
proved the LWP for a compressible gravity water wave with vorticity with a simplified method. In the case of nonzero surface tension, we refer to
[
15
,
23
,
24
]
. In the case of a gas, we refer to
[
16
,
20
,
40
,
64
,
33
,
39
]
and references therein.

Now let us review the development of elastodynamics system which describe the motion of an elastic medium. When the domain is fixed or
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, most of the results focus on the incompressible visco-elastic case because the solution is expected to be global. See
[
50
,
54
,
55
,
48
,
21
,
53
,
19
]
and references therein. For the compressible viscoelastic case, we refer to
[
36
,
37
,
38
,
72
,
73
]
. The incompressible ideal elastodynamic equations satisfy the null condition and thus the global solution in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
can also be expected. We refer to
[
27
,
77
,
78
,
49
,
47
,
86
,
6
]
.

However, the study of free-surface elastodynamics becomes quite different. On the one hand, the domain is no longer
\mathbb{R}^{n}
but with a boundary whose regularity is also limited and thus it is quite difficult to recover the global solution as in the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
-case. The only related result is Xu-Zhang-Zhang
[
90
]
that proved the global well-posedness (GWP) of incompressible visco-elastodynamics with surface tension. On the other hand, the contribution of free boundary enters to the highest order term in the energy, and extra stabilizing conditions such as Rayleigh-Taylor sign condtion are required. So far, nearly all the known results only deal with the local-in-time a priori estimates and LWP for the neo-Hookean elastodynamics and most are only available for the incompressible case. For the incompressible case, Hao-Wang
[
34
]
proved the Christodoulou-Lindblad type a priori estimates under Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition (
1.8
). Gu-Wang
[
30
]
and Li-Wang-Zhang
[
52
]
proved the LWP under a mixed stability condition. Li-Wang-Zhang
[
51
]
proved the LWP for the incompressible vortex sheets in elastodynamics under a non-collinearity condition. Hu-Huang
[
35
]
proved the LWP under Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition by generalizing Lindblad
[
56
,
58
]
, and Zhang
[
94
]
gives an alternative proof by generalizing Gu-Wang
[
31
]
in incompressible MHD. In the case of nonzero surface tension, Gu-Lei
[
29
]
proved the LWP by vanishing viscosity method.

Futher difficulty arises in the compressible case compared with the incompressible case due to the coupling of pressure wave and the motion of elastic medium. The only known result on the free-boundary problem in elastodynamics is Trakhinin
[
85
]
that proved the LWP under either the non-collinearity condition
1
1

1

The non-collinearity condition reads
|\mathbf{F}_{j}\times\mathbf{F}_{k}|\geq\delta>0
for
k\neq j
. This non-collinearity condition allows us to express the gradient of the flow map in terms of the deformation tensor, and thus actually enhances extra 1/2-order regularity of the free-boundary than being under Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition.
or Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition. The proof in
[
85
]
relies on the Nash-Moser iteration and thus cannot get energy estimates without loss of regularity. Trakhinin
[
85
]
also pointed out that the ill-posedness happens if the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition and the non-collinearity condition fail simultaneously.

As stated in the remark after (
1.4
), the free boundary problem can be considered as the one-phase problem of the vortex sheets in compressible elastodynamics. For that, Chen-Hu-Wang
[
8
,
9
]
proved the linearized stability of vortex sheets in 2D compressible elastodynamics and Chen-Hu-Wang-Yuan
[
11
]
for the 3D case. Chen-Hu-Wang-Wang-Yuan
[
10
]
also proved the nonlinear stability in 2D. We also mention that Chen-Secchi-Wang
[
7
]
proved the linear stability of contact discontinuity. Morando-Trakhinin-Trebeschi
[
69
]
proved the structural stability of shocks in compressible elastodynamics. Among all these results, the proofs strongly rely on Nash-Moser iteration.
So far, no nonlinear energy estimate without loss of regularity is available for the free-boundary compressible elastodynamics system
.

The other topic considered in this manuscript is the incompressible limit. In physics, the incompressible limit helps people to understand the property of a slightly compressible fluid via its incompressible counterpart and vice versa. In the mathematical study of inviscid fluid, most results on the incompressible limit or slightly compressible fluid focus on the Euler equations in fixed domain or
\mathbb{R}^{n}

[
42
,
43
,
25
,
74
,
76
,
68
,
2
,
12
,
13
,
22
]
and reference therein. The incompressible limit method was also used in elastodynamics
[
77
,
50
,
61
]
. In the study of the incompressible limit of a free-surface fluid, only quite few results are available. See Lindblad-Luo
[
60
]
for compressible Euler, Luo
[
62
]
for compressible water wave, Disconzi-Luo
[
24
]
for the nonzero surface tension case and the author
[
91
]
for compressible resistive MHD. The study of incompressible limit of free-boundary compressible elastodynamics is still open.

In the presenting manuscript, we prove the local well-posedness of the free-boundary compressible ideal elastodynamics system under Rayleigh-Taylor sign conditon (
1.8
) and the energy estimate without regularity loss. The energy estimates are also uniform in sound speed and thus yield the incompressible limit. Our proof no longer relies on higher order wave equation as in previous work
[
60
,
62
,
91
]
and also applies to Euler equations. Besides, we are able to recover the higher order energy established in
[
60
,
62
,
91
]
for a slightly compressible elastic medium. To the best of our knowledge, we first establish the energy estimates without loss of regularity and the incompressible limit of the free-boundary compressible elastodynamics system under Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition. See Section
1.3
for detailed strategy of the proof.

1.2
Reformulation in Lagrangian coordinates and main results

We introduce Lagrangian coordinates to reduce the free-boundary problem to a fixed-domain problem. We introduce the reference domain
2
2

2

The reference domain allows us to work in one coordinate patch and the result for a general simply-connect domain follows from partition of unity. See Coutand-Shkoller
[
17
,
18
]
for details.

\Omega:=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(-1,1)
with boundary
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(\{-1\}\cup\{1\})
. Denote the coordinates on
\Omega
by
y=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
and the spatial derivative in the Lagrangian coordiantes by
\partial=\partial_{y}
. We define
\eta:[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathcal{D}
to be the flow map of the velocity
u
by

\partial_{t}\eta(t,y)=u(t,\eta(t,y)),~~~\eta(0,y)=\eta_{0}(y),

where
\eta_{0}:\Omega\to\mathcal{D}_{0}
is a diffeomorphism. For technical simplicity, we take
\eta_{0}=\text{Id}
, i.e., we assume the initial domian
\mathcal{D}_{0}=\Omega=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(0,1)
. In fact, our proof in the manuscript is also applicable to the case for general data
\eta_{0}\in H^{4}
. By the chain rule, one can verify that
\partial_{t}(f(t,\eta(t,y))=(D_{t}f)(t,\eta(t,y))
, i.e., the material derivative becomes time derivative in Lagrangian coordinates.

Next we introduce the Lagrangian variables

v(t,y):=u(t,\eta(t,y)),~h(t,y):=\mathfrak{h}(t,\eta(t,y)),~F_{ij}(t,y):=\mathbf{F}_{ij}(t,\eta(t,y)),~F_{j}(t,y):=\mathbf{F}_{j}(t,\eta(t,y)),

the co-factor matrix
a:=[\partial\eta]^{-1}
by
a^{li}=a^{l}_{i}:=\frac{\partial y^{l}}{\partial x^{i}}
where
x^{i}:=\eta^{i}(t,y)
is the
i
-th component of Eulerian coordinate, the Jacobian determinant
J:=\det[\partial\eta]
and
A:=Ja
. These quantities are always well-defined because
\eta
is around the identity map when
t
is small. Then the free-boundary compressible elastodynamic system (
1.6
)-(
1.7
) can be re-written in Lagrangian coordinates as

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{a}h+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(F_{j}\cdot\nabla_{a})F_{j}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }_{a}v=-e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}h~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}F_{j}=(F_{j}\cdot\nabla_{a})v~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
(\text{div }_{a}F^{\top})_{j}=-e^{\prime}(h)(F_{j}\cdot\nabla_{a})h~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}|_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{T}([0,T]\times\Gamma)~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
h=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
F_{j}\cdot N=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
-\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\geq c_{0}>0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h,F)|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0},\mathbf{F}^{0}).\end{cases}

(1.11)

Here
N=(0,0,\pm 1)
is the exterior unit normal on the boundary
\Gamma:=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\{\pm 1\}
, and
(\nabla_{a}f)^{i}:=a^{li}\partial_{l}f
. Note that the divergence constraint on
F
and Taylor sign condition are only required for initial data, so the system (
1.11
) is not over-determined.

There are several important geometric identities in Lagrangian coordinates. We have Piola’s identity
\partial_{l}A^{li}=0
for any
i
, and

Da^{li}=-a^{lr}\partial_{m}D\eta_{r}a^{mi},~~D=\partial\text{ or }\partial_{t},

and
\partial_{t}J=J\text{div }_{a}v
. With the help of these identities, one can express the deformation tensor as the
\mathbf{F}^{0}
-directional derivative of the flow map. We compute for any
j

\partial_{t}(F_{ij}a^{li})=\partial_{t}F_{ij}a^{li}+F_{ij}\partial_{t}a^{li}=F_{kj}a^{mk}\partial_{m}v_{i}a^{li}-F_{ij}a^{lr}\partial_{m}v_{r}a^{mi}=0,

which yields
F_{ij}a^{li}=\mathbf{F}^{0}_{ij}\delta^{li}=\mathbf{F}^{0}_{lj}
and thus

F_{kj}=F_{ij}a^{li}\partial_{l}\eta_{k}=\mathbf{F}^{0}_{lj}\partial_{l}\eta_{k}=(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta_{k}.

(1.12)

From now on,
\text{div }Y:=\partial_{i}Y^{i}
denotes the standard Lagrangian divergence instead of the Eulerian one. Under this setting, the divergence constraint becomes

\text{div }({\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{0}_{kj}=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\mathfrak{h}_{0}.

(1.13)

Therefore, the system (
1.11
) can be further simplified to the following system.

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{a}h+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }_{a}v=-e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}h~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }({\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{0}_{kj}=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\mathfrak{h}_{0}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}|_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{T}([0,T]\times\Gamma)~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
h=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot N=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
-\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\geq c_{0}>0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h)|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0}).\end{cases}

(1.14)

We aim to prove the local well-posedness and the incompressible limit of the system (
1.14
), i.e., the free-boundary compressible elastodynamic system in Lagrangian coordinates. Before stating the main results, we introduce our energy functional as follows. Here we denote
\|f\|_{s}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}
for any function
f(t,y)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Omega
and
|f|_{s}:=|f(t,\cdot)|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}
for any function
f(t,y)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma
.

Definition 1.1
.

Define energy functional
\mathbb{E}
at time
T
to be

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(T)

\displaystyle:=\left\|\eta\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{4}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left|a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}

(1.15)

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{t}v\right\|_{3}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}\eta\right\|_{3}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{t}h\right\|_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\right\|_{2}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{2}\eta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{2}h\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}v\right\|_{1}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{3}\eta\right\|_{1}^{2}+\left\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{3}h\right\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}v\right\|_{0}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{4}\eta\right\|_{0}^{2}+\left\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{\frac{3}{2}}\partial_{t}^{4}h\right\|_{0}^{2}.

And define higher order energy functional
\mathfrak{E}(T)
to be

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(T):=\mathbb{E}(T)+\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{4}v\right\|_{1}^{2}+\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{4}\left((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\right)\right\|_{1}^{2}+\left|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}\partial_{t}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}+\left\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{\frac{3}{2}}\partial_{t}^{5}h\right\|_{0}^{2}+\left\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}h\right\|_{1}^{2}.

(1.16)

The main results in this manuscript are listed in the following 4 theorems.

1. Local well-posedness

Theorem 1.1
.

Given initial data
v_{0},\mathbf{F}^{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0}\in H^{4}(\Omega)
satisfying the compatibility conditions (
1.10
) up to 4-th order
3
3

3

The reason for requiring 4-th order is that
\partial_{t}^{4}h
appears in the boundary integral in the proof.
and the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition (
1.8
), there exists some
T>0
depending only on the initial data, such that the system (
1.14
) has a unique strong solution
(\eta,v,h)
with the energy estimates

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathbb{E}(t)\leq P\left(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|\mathfrak{h}_{0}\|_{4}\right),

(1.17)

where
P(\cdots)
represents a polynomial in its arguments.

\square

2. Incompressible limit

We parametrize the sound speed by parametre
\varepsilon>0
in such that

p^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\rho)|_{\rho=1}=\varepsilon.

We consider the compressible elastodynamic equations with variable
(\eta^{\varepsilon},v^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon},h^{\varepsilon})
if the sound speed satisfies
p^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(\rho)|_{\rho=1}=\varepsilon
. Under this setting, the density
\rho^{\varepsilon}(h)\to 1
and thus the enthalpy
e(h^{\varepsilon})
converges to 0 as
\varepsilon\to\infty
. The above energy estimates are actually uniform in the sound speed, i.e., the energy bound of
\mathbb{E}(T)
does not rely on
e^{\prime}(h)^{-1}
.

For every
\varepsilon>0
, let
(v_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\varepsilon})\in H^{4}(\Omega)\times H^{4}(\Omega)\times H^{4}(\Omega)
be the initial data with sound speed
\varepsilon
of the free-boundary compressible elastodynamic system (
1.14
) satisfying the compatibility conditions up to 4-th order. Then we can establish the incompressible limit in the following sense:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\textbf{v}_{0}\in H^{4}(\Omega)
be a divergence-free vector field and
\mathbf{G}^{0}\in H^{4}(\Omega)
be a divergence-free matrix in the sense of
\partial_{k}\mathbf{G}^{0}_{kj}=0
for all
j
. Define
Q_{0}
be the solution to the elliptic equation with constraints
-\frac{\partial Q}{\partial N}|_{\Gamma}\geq c_{0}>0

\begin{cases}-\Delta Q_{0}=\partial_{i}\textbf{v}_{0}^{k}\partial_{k}\textbf{v}_{0}^{i}-\partial_{i}\mathbf{G}^{0}_{kj}\partial_{k}\mathbf{G}^{0}_{ij}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
Q_{0}=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma.\\
\end{cases}

Let
(V,G,Q)
be the solution to the free-boundary incompressible elastodynamic system with initial data
(\textbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{G}^{0},Q_{0})\in H^{4}(\Omega)\times H^{4}(\Omega)\times H^{4}(\Omega)
:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=V~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}V=-\nabla_{a}Q+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(G\cdot\nabla_{a})G~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }_{a}V=0~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
(\text{div }G^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}G_{kj}=0~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}|_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{T}([0,T]\times\Gamma)~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
Q=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
G_{j}\cdot N=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
-\frac{\partial Q}{\partial N}\geq c_{0}>0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,V,G,Q)|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},\textbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{G}^{0},Q_{0}).\end{cases}

(1.18)

Suppose also there exists a sequence of initial data of (
1.11
)
(v_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}^{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{C^{1}}(\textbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{G}^{0},Q_{0})
as
\varepsilon\to\infty
and satisfies the compatibility conditions up to 4-th order. Then there exist some
T_{0}>0
independent of
\varepsilon
such that

1.

The corresponding energy functionals
\mathbb{E}^{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{E}^{\varepsilon}
are bounded uniformly in
\varepsilon
in
[0,T_{0}]
.

2.

The corresponding solutions
(v^{\varepsilon},F^{\varepsilon},h^{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{C^{1}}(V,G,Q)
as
\varepsilon\to\infty
in
[0,T_{0}]
.

\square

Remark
.

In fact, as
\varepsilon\to\infty
(i.e.,
e^{\prime}(h^{\varepsilon})\to 0
),
\mathbb{E}(T)
and
\mathfrak{E}(T)
converge exactly to the energy functional of the free-boundary incompressible elastodynamic system under Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition
-\partial_{3}Q\geq c_{0}^{\prime}>0
established by Zhang
[
94
]

\mathbb{E}^{\infty}:=\left\|\eta\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left\|V\right\|_{4}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{G}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left\|Q\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left|a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}.

The reason is that one can invoke the second equation to reduce time derivatives of
v
to spatial derivatives of
h
and
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
and the weighted terms converge to zero.

3. Enhanced regularity in the slightly compressible case

In Lindblad-Luo
[
60
]
and Luo
[
62
]
on the compressible Euler equations, they required the
H^{1}
-control of
\partial_{t}^{4}
-derivatives to close the energy estimates and also the incompressible limit. In Theorem
1.1
and
1.2
we are able to drop that higher order energy. Moreover, we are still able to prove such higher regularity of full time derivatives for the elastodynamics equations when the elastic medium is slightly compressible, i.e.,
|e^{\prime}(h)|\ll 1
is sufficiently small. The following result shows that our method are also applicable for compressible Euler equations and also recover the previous results
[
60
,
62
]
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose the initial data satisfies the compatibility conditions up to 5-th order. When the sound speed
\varepsilon
is sufficiently large, the solution
(v,F,h)
constructed in Theorem
1.1
satisfies higher order energy estimates uniform in
\epsilon
in
[0,T_{1}]
where
0<T_{1}<T
only depends on the initial data.

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T_{1}}\mathfrak{E}(t)\leq P\left(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|\mathfrak{h}_{0}\|_{4}\right).

(1.19)

\square

4. Existence of initial data satisfying the compatibility conditions

Finally, we proved the existence of initial data which satisfies the compatibility conditions up to 5-th order
4
4

4

The reason for requiring 5-th order is that
\partial_{t}^{5}h
appears on the boundary integral in the analysis of enhanced regularity.
and strongly converges to the incompressible data as the sound speed goes to infinity. Define
M_{0}:=\|v_{0}\|_{4}^{2}+\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4}^{2}+\|\mathfrak{h}_{0}\|_{4}^{2}
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Given the initial data
(\textbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{G}^{0},Q_{0})\in H^{5}\times H^{5}\times H^{5}
of the incompressible elastodynamics system (
1.18
), there exist initial data
(v_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\varepsilon})\in H^{5}\times H^{5}\times H^{5}
of (
1.11
) such that

1.

(v_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\varepsilon})\xrightarrow{C^{1}}(\textbf{v}_{0},\mathbf{G}^{0},Q_{0})
as
\varepsilon\to+\infty
.

2.

(v_{0}^{\varepsilon},{\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\varepsilon})
satisfies the compatibility conditions (
1.10
) up to 5-th order.

3.

The energy functionals satisfy
\mathbb{E}(0)+\mathfrak{E}(0)\leq P(M_{0})
.

\square

1.3
Strategy of the proof

Our strategy is different from the previous works on either compressible Euler equations or incompressible elastodynamic equations. In particular, the simultaneous presence of deformation tensor and compressibility makes the fixed-point argument in
[
17
,
18
,
28
,
63
,
30
,
31
,
94
]
no longer applicable to solve the linearized system even if one has the a priori estimates without regularity loss. Now we give illustrations on the strategy of the proof. Keep in mind that
\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot N|_{\Gamma}=0
implies
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)
is a tangential derivative on the boundary.

1.3.1
Control of the energy functional

Let us temporarily focus on the energy estimates of the original equation (
1.14
) instead of constructing the approximation solution. The first step is div-curl-tangential decomposition

\displaystyle\|v\|_{4}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|v\|_{0}+\|\text{curl }v\|_{3}+\|\text{div }v\|_{3}+|\overline{\partial}{}v\cdot N|_{5/2},

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{0}+\|\text{curl }(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{3}+\|\text{div }(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{3}+|\overline{\partial}{}((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)\cdot N|_{5/2}.

The curl estimates can be directly controlled via the evolution equation. The boundary part can be reduced to interior tangential estimates and divergence estimates by the normal trace lemma (cf. Lemma
2.3
). To estimate
\|\overline{\partial}{}^{4}v\|_{0}
and
\|\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{0}
, one cannot directly take
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
in the equation
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{a}h+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta
and compute the
L^{2}
-type estimates because this requires the control of
\|[\overline{\partial}{}^{4},a^{li}]\partial_{l}h\|_{0}
where
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}a\approx\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\partial\eta\times\partial\eta
cannot be controlled in
L^{2}
. For Euler equations
[
17
,
18
]
one can integrate
\overline{\partial}{}^{1/2}
by parts and control
\|\text{curl }\eta\|_{7/2}
, but this is no longer applicable to elastodynamics equations: The preserved property of irrotationality for Euler equations, which is the key to the enhanced regularity of flow map
\eta
than
v
, no longer holds for elastodynamics equations. Instead,
\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{9/2}
is necessary for
\|\text{curl }\eta\|_{7/2}
but impossible for us to control.

To overcome such difficulty, we use Alinhac good unknown method which reveals that the “essential” highest order term in
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\nabla_{a}f)
should be the covariant derivative of the “Alinhac good unknown” instead of simply commute
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
with
\nabla_{a}
. In other words, the main idea is to rewrite
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\nabla_{a}f)
to be the sum of convariance part
\nabla_{a}\mathsf{F}
plus an
L^{2}(\Omega)
-bounded term
C(f)
such that

\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\nabla_{a}f)=\nabla_{a}\mathsf{F}+C(f),\text{ with }\|\mathsf{F}-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}f\|_{0}+\|\partial_{t}(\mathsf{F}-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}f)\|_{0}+\|C(f)\|_{0}\leq P(\mathbb{E}(t)).

The quantity
\mathsf{F}
is called the “Alinhac good unknown” of
f
with respect to
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
. That is to say, Alinhac’s good unknowns allow us to take into account the covariance under the change of coordinates to avoid the extra regularity assumption on the flow map.

To derive the precise form of
\mathsf{F}
, one can use chain rule
\overline{\partial}{}_{i}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}=\dfrac{\partial\eta^{\alpha}}{\partial y^{i}}\dfrac{\partial}{\partial\eta^{\alpha}}=\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}
:

\nabla_{a}(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}f)=\nabla_{a}\left((\overline{\partial}{}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a})(\overline{\partial}{}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a})(\overline{\partial}{}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a})(\overline{\partial}{}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a})f\right)=\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\nabla_{a}f)+\nabla_{a}(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}f)+\text{l.o.t.}.\

It is not difficult to find that the term
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}f
also enters to the highest order, which should be merged into the covariance part. Therefore,
\mathsf{F}:=\overline{\partial}{}^{4}f-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}f
satisfies our requirement. Such crucial fact was first observed by Alinhac
[
4
]
, and then widely used in the study of first-order quasilinear hyperbolic system. In the study of free-surface fluid, this was first implicity applied by Christodoulou-Lindblad
[
14
]
, and later there are some explicit applications such as
[
65
,
87
,
31
,
30
,
94
,
63
,
92
]
.

Now we introduce the Alinhac good unknowns
\mathbf{V}:=\overline{\partial}{}^{4}v-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}v
and
\mathbf{H}:=\overline{\partial}{}^{4}h-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}h
and get the evolution equations. Below,
\cdots
stands for controllable terms.

\partial_{t}\mathbf{V}+\nabla_{a}\mathbf{H}-\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\left(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\right)=\cdots,

(1.20)

subject to

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}\cdot\mathbf{V}=\overline{\partial}{}^{4}(\text{div }_{a}v)+C(v)\text{ in }\Omega\text{ and }\mathbf{H}=(-\partial_{3}h)a^{3k}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{k}\text{ on }\Gamma,

(1.21)

where
C(f)
is defined to be the error terms satisfying
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\nabla_{a}f=\nabla_{a}(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}f-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta\cdot\nabla_{a}f)+C(f)
and is controllable. Then the standard
L^{2}
-estimate of (
1.20
) gives the boundary integral contributed by the free surface

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{k}a^{3k}a^{3i}\mathbf{V}_{i}\,dS=\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{k}a^{3k}a^{3i}(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\partial_{t}\eta_{i}-\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{r}a^{lr}\partial_{l}v_{i})\,dS

(1.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\left|a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|^{2}\,dS+\cdots

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}a^{3k}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{k}\partial_{t}a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\,dS-\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{k}a^{3k}a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{r}a^{lr}\partial_{l}v_{i}\,dS.

Invoking the Taylor sign condition (
1.8
) we get the boundary energy
\left|a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}
. Then plugging
\partial_{t}a^{3i}=-a^{3r}\partial_{l}v_{r}a^{li}
into the second term yields the cancellation structure: The last two terms in (
1.22
) can be controlled by the boundary energy when
l=3
, and exactly cancel each other when
l=1,2
.

Key observation: Structure of wave equation of
h

From (
1.14
) we can deduce that
\text{div }_{a}v=-e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}h
and
\text{div }_{a}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta=-e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)h
. This motivates us to consider the wave equation of
h
derived by taking
\text{div }_{a}
in the second equation of (
1.14
)

\displaystyle e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{2}h-\Delta_{a}h=e^{\prime}(h)\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h+\text{quadratic terms of first order derivative}.

(1.23)

Although the RHS of (
1.23
) has a potential to lose regularity due to the same order of derivatives, we are still able to do the energy estimates thanks to special structure of
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h
. If we take
L^{2}
inner product of (
1.23
) and
\partial_{t}h
, the LHS gives the energy terms
\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}e^{\prime}(h)|\partial_{t}h|^{2}+|\nabla_{a}h|^{2}\,dy
containing
\text{div }_{a}v
. The RHS now becomes

\int_{\Omega}e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h\cdot\partial_{t}h=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}e^{\prime}(h)|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)h|^{2}\,dy+\cdots,

which exactly gives the control of
\text{div }_{a}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
. However, the
H^{3}
-control of
\text{div }_{a}v
and
\text{div }_{a}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
is not a direct result of the
L^{2}
-control of
\partial^{3}
-differentiated wave equation (
1.23
) because integrating the Laplacian term by parts yields a boundary integral which cannot be controlled due to the presence of normal derivatives.

Our idea to overcome this difficulty is to use Christodoulou-Lindblad type elliptic estimates in Lagrangian coordinates (Lemma
2.6
) and invoke (
1.23
) to replace two normal derivatives of
h
by two tangential derivatives. Repeatedly, the estimates of
h
can be reduced to tangential derivatives of
h,\partial h
which can be controlled by tangentially-differentiated wave equation (
1.23
). Specifically, we start with

\|h\|_{4}\approx\|\nabla_{a}h\|_{3}\lesssim P(\|\eta\|_{3})(\|\Delta_{a}h\|_{2}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\eta\|_{3}\|h\|_{3}).

Then invoking (
1.23
) we can reduce
\|\Delta_{a}h\|_{2}
to
\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{2}
and
\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h\|_{2}
plus lower order terms. Note that
\partial_{t}
and
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)
are tangential derivatives, so we gain 2 tangential derivatives once we proceed such a step. Then we can do the same thing for
\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{2}
and
\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h\|_{2}
. For example,

\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{2}\approx\|e^{\prime}(h)\nabla_{a}\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{1}\lesssim P(\|\eta\|_{2})(\|e^{\prime}(h)\Delta_{a}\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{0}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\eta\|_{2}\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{0}),

then plugging the
\partial_{t}^{2}
-differentiated wave equations (
1.23
) reduces
\|e^{\prime}(h)\Delta_{a}\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{0}
to
\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{2}\partial_{t}^{4}h\|_{0}
and
\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h\|_{0}
which are purely tangential derivatives of
h
.

In summary, we have the following reduction procedure

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{2}\partial_{t}^{4}h\|_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|h\|_{4}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h\|_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j,k=1}^{3}\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{k}\cdot\partial)^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}h\|_{0},}

(1.24)

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{3}h\|_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{2}h\|_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\|\partial_{t}h\|_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)h\|_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\partial_{t}h\|_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sum\limits_{j,k=1}^{3}\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{k}\cdot\partial)^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)h\|_{1}.}

(1.25)

Therefore, (
1.24
) and (
1.25
) show that it suffices to control
\partial_{t}^{3}
-differentiated,
\partial_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)
-differentiated,
\partial_{t}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}
-differentiated and
\sum\limits_{k=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{k}\cdot\partial)^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)
-differentiated wave equation (
1.23
), whose weighted
L^{2}
-estimates exactly give the energy terms that we want. See Section
3.4
for detailed computation.

The above analysis gives the control of divergence and thus finalize the control of full spatial derivatives. It now remains to control the time derivatives of
v
and
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
. We can invoke
\partial_{t}v=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)-\nabla_{a}h
and
\partial_{t}((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)=(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)v
repeatedly to replace time derivatives by spatial derivatives until there is no time derivative.

1.3.2
Tangentially-smoothed approximation problem

1. Tangential smoothing: Necessity and adjusted mollification

With the a priori estimates of (
1.14
), it is straightforward to consider the standard iteration to prove the well-posedness. Specifically, if one starts with trivial solution
(\eta^{(0)},v^{(0)},h^{(0)})=(\text{Id},0,0)
and inductively define
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})
by

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta^{(n+1)}=v^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v^{(n+1)}=-\nabla_{{a^{(n)}}}h^{(n+1)}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{a^{(n)}}v^{(n+1)}=-e^{\prime}(h^{(n)})\partial_{t}h^{(n+1)}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
h^{(n+1)}=0~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta^{(n+1)},v^{(n+1)},h^{(n+1)})|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},h_{0})\end{cases}

(1.26)

where
a^{(n)}:=[\partial\eta^{(n)}]^{-1}
, then we have to control
\|v\|_{4}
when solving the linearized system where
\|\nabla_{{a^{(n)}}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{4}
is required. Invoking Lemma
2.6
yields

\|\nabla_{{a^{(n)}}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{4}\lesssim P(\|\eta^{(n)}\|_{4})\left(\|\Delta_{a^{(n)}}h^{(n+1)}\|_{3}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\eta^{(n+1)}\|_{4}\|h^{(n+1)}\|_{4}\right),

(1.27)

where
\|\overline{\partial}{}\eta^{(n+1)}\|_{4}
loses one tangential derivative. Another difficulty appears in the uniform-in-
n
estimates of the linearized system (
1.26
) which is necessary for iteration. The boundary integral (
1.22
) appearing in the tangential estimates now becomes

\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h^{(n+1)}}{\partial N}\underbrace{\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\eta_{k}^{(n)}}_{n\text{-th solution}}a^{(n)3k}a^{(n)3i}(\underbrace{\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\eta^{(n+1)}_{i}}_{(n+1)\text{-th solution}}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\eta_{k}^{(n)}a^{(n)lk}\partial_{l}v^{(n+1)})\,dS

(1.28)

which no longer exhibits the cancellation structure or produces the boundary energy term
\left|a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}
.

Note that such derivative losses are both tangential and even appear in the study of Euler equations
[
17
,
18
,
20
,
28
,
63
]
. This motivates people to tangentially mollify the coefficients
a
to compensate the loss of regularity. Such tangential smoothing method is first introduced by Coutand-Shkoller
[
17
,
18
]
. Define
\Lambda_{\kappa}
to be the standard mollifier with parametre
\kappa>0
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as in (
2.4
). In
[
17
,
18
]
, they defined
\tilde{\eta}:=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta
and
\tilde{a}=[\partial\tilde{\eta}]^{-1}
and then defined the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation problem by replacing
a
with
\tilde{a}
. However, such construction is not applicable to the elastodynamics system because we have to control the term
\|[(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial),\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}]\eta\|_{4}
in which there is a normal derivative
\mathbf{F}^{0}_{3j}\partial_{3}
. Motivated by Gu-Wang
[
31
]
, we can mollify
\eta
on the boundary first, then do the harmonic extensin to the interior

\begin{cases}-\Delta\tilde{\eta}=-\Delta\eta~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\tilde{\eta}=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma.\end{cases}

(1.29)

Denote
\tilde{a}:=[\partial\tilde{\eta}]^{-1}
to be the cofactor matrix,
\tilde{J}:=\det[\partial\tilde{\eta}]
to be the Jacobian determinant and
\tilde{A}:=\tilde{J}\tilde{a}
. Then we define the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation system of (
1.14
) to be

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v+\psi~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v=-e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}h~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }({\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{0}_{kj}=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\mathfrak{h}_{0}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}|_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{T}([0,T]\times\Gamma)~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
h=0,\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot N=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
-\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\geq c_{0}>0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h)|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0}).\end{cases}

(1.30)

Here the term
\psi=\psi(\eta,v)
is a correction term which solves the following Laplacian equation

\begin{cases}\Delta\psi=0&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\psi=\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}\sum\limits_{L=1}^{2}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{l}\tilde{a}^{Lk}\overline{\partial}{}_{L}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{k}\tilde{a}^{Lk}\overline{\partial}{}_{L}v\right)&~~~\text{on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(1.31)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}
denotes the standard Littlewood-Paley projection in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
which removes the zero-frequency part.
\overline{\Delta}{}:=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
denotes the tangential Laplacian operator. The special structure of
\psi
also indicates that it is more convenient to replace
\overline{\partial}{}^{4}
by
\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}
in the tangential estimates.

We emphasize that it seems necessary to add a correction term
\psi
defined in (
1.31
) to the flow map equation. The reason is that the mollification breaks the cancellation structure in the boundary integral (
1.22
), which now becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{k}\tilde{a}^{3k}\tilde{a}^{3i}(\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\partial_{t}\eta_{i}-\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{r}\tilde{a}^{lr}\partial_{l}v_{i})\,dS

(1.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\left|\tilde{a}^{3i}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{i}\right|^{2}\,dS+\cdots

\displaystyle+\sum_{L=1}^{2}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\tilde{a}^{3k}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{k}\tilde{a}^{3r}\overline{\partial}{}_{L}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v_{r}\tilde{a}^{Li}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{i}\,dS-\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\tilde{a}^{3k}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{k}\tilde{a}^{3i}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{r}a^{Lr}\partial_{L}v_{i}\,dS\right),

of which the last line is zero when
\kappa=0
according to the cancellation structure (
1.22
) but cannot be controlled when
\kappa>0
. In Ginsberg-Lindblad-Luo
[
28
]
they introduced
\kappa^{2}
-weighted higher order terms in the energy functional which made the proof quite complicated. Here, such term can be exactly cancelled with the contribution of
\psi
on
\Gamma

\int_{\Gamma}\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\tilde{a}^{3i}\tilde{a}^{3k}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\tilde{\eta}_{k}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\psi_{i}\,dS.

Remark
.

After introducing the correction term
\psi
, we no longer have
\partial_{t}\eta=v
. We note that the contribution of
\psi
is controllable and is analyzed in Lemma
3.2
and Section
3.4.2
. Therefore, we are able to follow the strategy in Section
1.3.1
to prove the uniform-in-
\kappa
a priori estimates of (
1.30
).

1.3.3
Construction of solutions to the approximation system

To prove the local well-posedness of (
1.14
), it now remains to construct the unique strong solution to the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation problem (
1.30
) for each
fixed

\kappa>0
. According to the analysis above, it now seems hopeful to proceed the linearization and fixed-point argument because the coefficient
a
has been mollified to be a
C^{\infty}
-function
\tilde{a}
. Let us consider the linearized
\kappa
-approximation system (
4.2
)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v+\mathring{\psi}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{\mathring{\tilde{a}}}h+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{\mathring{\tilde{a}}}v=-e^{\prime}(\mathring{h})\partial_{t}h~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}:=\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{0}_{kj}=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\mathfrak{h}_{0}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
h=0,~\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot N=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h)|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0}),\end{cases}

(1.33)

where the variables marked with “ring” on top denotes the
n
-th approximation solution in the iteration scheme and the others correspond to the
(n+1)
-th approximation solution.

1. Failure of fixed-point argument due to compressibility and deformation tensor

Unfortunately, the fixed-point argument seems not applicable to solve the linearized system (
1.33
) even if we could obtain the a priori estimates without loss of regularity. The presence of Cauchy-Green tensor
\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta
makes the second equation of (
1.33
) lose one derivative in the fixed-point argument. This tells an essential difficulty in elastodynamics that the Euler equations never have. In Gu-Wang
[
31
]
and Zhang
[
94
]
, they introduced a directional viscosity term in flow map to compensate the derivative loss:

\partial_{t}\eta-\mu\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta=v+\mathring{\psi}.

But the estimate for vanishing viscosity process strongly relies on the incompressibility assumptions. In other words, the compressibility stands for another essential difficulty compared with the study of Euler equations and incompressible elastodynamics. See detailed analysis in Section
4.1
. Therefore, it is not possible for us to deal with the compressible elastodynamic system by merely technical modifying the methods in the study of compressible Euler equations
[
60
,
28
,
63
]
and incompressible MHD
[
31
]
or elastodynamics
[
94
]
.

2. Hyperbolic approach to solve the linearized
\kappa
-approximation system

We observe that if we replace the first equation of (
1.33
) by
\partial_{t}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta=(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)v+(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\mathring{\psi}
and consider
\mathfrak{F}_{j}:=(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
instead of
\eta
itself as an unknown of the linearized system, then the linearized system (
1.33
) becomes a first-order symmetric hyperbolic system with characteristic boundary conditions
[
46
,
67
]
and thus can be solved by the arguments in Lax-Phillips
[
46
]
. Note that once
\mathfrak{F},v,h
are solved, the flow map
\eta
is automatically solved by
\partial_{t}\eta=v+\mathring{\psi}
. After this, we can construct the solution of the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation system (
1.30
) for each fixed
\kappa>0
via standard Picard iteration and we refer to Section
4.2
for details. Finally, the uniform-in-
\kappa
estimate for (
1.30
) yields the local well-posedness and energy estimates of the original system (
1.14
). Theorem
1.1
is proven.

1.3.4
Incompressible limit and Construction of initial data

The second goal of the presenting manuscript is to establish the incompressible limit as stated in Theorem
1.2
. This directly follows from the energy estimates uniform in sound speed, for which we need to set the energy functional
\mathbb{E}(T)
to be
e^{\prime}(h)
-weighted by suitable choices of the power of weight functions. We note that our choices of weight functions in
\mathbb{E}(T)
come from the reduction procedures (
1.24
)-(
1.25
), and also help us avoid analyzing the higher order wave equation of
h
which are different from the related previous works Lindblad-Luo
[
60
]
, Luo
[
62
]
and the author
[
91
]
. Finally, we need to prove the existence of the initial data satisfying the compatibility conditions (
1.10
) up to 5-th order. See Section
8
for detailed description and construction. Once the initial datum are constructed, all the proofs of well-posedness and incompressible limit are finished.

1.3.5
Comparison with previous works on Euler equations

In the previous works of incompressible limit of compressible free-surface inviscid fluid
[
60
,
62
,
91
]
, the weighted
H^{1}
-norm of full time derivative of
h
is also required, i.e.,
\|\partial_{t}^{4}h\|_{1}
and
\|\partial_{t}^{5}h\|_{0}
. The reason is that the Christodoulou-Lindblad type energy functionals in
[
60
,
62
,
91
]
contain the boundary energy of time derivatives. Our work shows that such boundary energies are not necessary even if we require the energy to be uniform in sound speed. In other words, we show that the contribution of free boundary is presented by
\left|a^{3i}\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}
, exactly the tangential derivatives of the second fundamental form of the free boundary. This coincides with the core conclusion in
[
14
,
60
,
62
]
: The regularity and geometry of the free surface enters to the highest order. Hence, our proof is completely applicable to compressible Euler equations just by mathematically setting
\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{O}
and avoid analyzing unnecessary terms. Moreover, we are also able to recover the enhanced regularity of full time derivatives in
[
60
,
62
,
91
]
for a slightly compressible elastic medium. The proof method is completely different from
[
60
,
62
,
91
]
because we do not allow more spatial derivatives appearing in the estimates due to the presence of deformation tensor. Such difficulty can be overcome by further delicate analysis of Alinhac good unknowns and elliptic estimates. See Section
7
for details.

1.4
Organisation of the paper

In Section
2
we list preliminary lemmas which will be repeatedly used in the manuscript. In Section
3
we introduce the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation system (
3.2
) and prove the uniform-in-
\kappa
a priori estimates. Then we construct the solution to (
3.2
) by linearization, hyperbolic approach and Picard iteration in Section
4
. These two results together with the uniqueness prove the local well-posedness of the free-boundary compressible elastodynamics system (
1.14
) in Section
5
. Then we establish the incompressible limit in Section
6
, and recover the enhaced regularity of full time derivatives in Section
7
. Finally, the construction of initial data satisfying the compatibility conditions is shown in Section
8
.

List of Notations:

•

\Omega:=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(-1,1)
and
\Gamma:=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(\{\pm 1\})
.

•

\|\cdot\|_{s}
: We denote
\|f\|_{s}:=\|f(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{s}(\Omega)}
for any function
f(t,y)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Omega
.

•

|\cdot|_{s}
: We denote
|f|_{s}:=|f(t,\cdot)|_{H^{s}(\Gamma)}
for any function
f(t,y)\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Gamma
.

•

\|\cdot\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}
,
|\cdot|_{\dot{H}^{s}}
: Homogeneous Sobolev norm, replacing
H^{s}
above by
\dot{H}^{s}.

•

P(\cdots)
: A generic polynomial in its arguments;

•

\mathcal{P}_{0}
:
\mathcal{P}_{0}=P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|h_{0}\|_{4})
;

•

[T,f]g:=T(fg)-T(f)g
, and
[T,f,g]:=T(fg)-T(f)g-fT(g)
, where
T
denotes a differential operator or the mollifier and
f,g
are arbitrary functions.

•

\overline{\partial}{},\overline{\Delta}{}
:
\overline{\partial}{}=\partial_{1},\partial_{2}
denotes the tangential derivative and
\overline{\Delta}{}:=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
denotes the tangential Laplacian.

•

\nabla^{i}_{a}f:=a^{li}\partial_{l}f
,
\text{div }_{a}\mathbf{f}:=a^{li}\partial_{l}\mathbf{f}_{i}
and
(\text{curl }_{a}\mathbf{f})_{k}:=\epsilon_{kli}a^{ml}\partial_{m}\mathbf{f}^{i}
, where
\epsilon_{kli}
is the sign of the 3-permutation
(kli)\in S_{3}.

2
Preliminary lemmas

We need the following lemmas in this manuscript.

2.1
Sobolev inequalities

Lemma 2.1

(
Kato-Ponce type inequalities
)
.

Let
J=(I-\Delta)^{1/2},~s\geq 0
. Then the following estimates hold:

(1)
\forall s\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\|J^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{s,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}}+\|f\|_{L^{q_{1}}}\|g\|_{W^{s,q_{2}}},

(2.1)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{s}(fg)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{L^{p_{2}}}+\|f\|_{L^{q_{1}}}\|g\|_{\dot{W}^{s,q_{2}}},

with
1/2=1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}=1/q_{1}+1/q_{2}
and
2\leq p_{1},q_{2}<\infty
;

(2)
\forall s\geq 1
, we have

\|J^{s}(fg)-(J^{s}f)g-f(J^{s}g)\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\|f\|_{W^{1,p_{1}}}\|g\|_{W^{s-1,q_{2}}}+\|f\|_{W^{s-1,q_{1}}}\|g\|_{W^{1,q_{2}}}

(2.2)

for all the
1<p<p_{1},p_{2},q_{1},q_{2}<\infty
with
1/p_{1}+1/p_{2}=1/q_{1}+1/q_{2}=1/p
.

Proof.

See Kato-Ponce
[
41
]
.
∎

Lemma 2.2

(
Trace theorem for harmonic functions
)
.

Suppose that
s\geq 0.5
and
u
solves the boundary-valued problem

\begin{cases}\Delta u=0~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u=g~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{cases}

where
g\in H^{s}(\Gamma)
. Then it holds that

|g|_{s}\lesssim\|u\|_{s+0.5}\lesssim|g|_{s}

Proof.

The LHS follows from the standard Sobolev trace lemma, while the RHS is the property of Poisson integral, which can be found in
[
83
, Proposition 5.1.7]
.
∎

Lemma 2.3

(
Normal trace theorem
)
.

It holds that for a vector field
X

\left|\overline{\partial}{}X\cdot N\right|_{-0.5}\lesssim\|\overline{\partial}{}X\|_{0}+\|\text{div }X\|_{0}

(2.3)

Proof.

The proof directly follows from testing by a
H^{0.5}(\Gamma)
function and divergence theorem. See
[
31
, Lemma 3.4]
.
∎

2.2
Properties of tangential smoothing operator

Let
\zeta=\zeta(y_{1},y_{2})\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a standard cut-off function such that
\text{Spt }\zeta=\overline{B(0,1)}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2},~~0\leq\zeta\leq 1
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\zeta=1
. The corresponding dilation is

\zeta_{\kappa}(y_{1},y_{2})=\frac{1}{\kappa^{2}}\zeta\left(\frac{y_{1}}{\kappa},\frac{y_{2}}{\kappa}\right),~~\kappa>0.

Now we define

\Lambda_{\kappa}f(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\zeta_{\kappa}(y_{1}-z_{1},y_{2}-z_{2})f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\,dz_{1}\,dz_{2}.

(2.4)

The following lemma records the basic properties of tangential smoothing.

Lemma 2.4

(
Regularity and Commutator estimates
)
.

For
\kappa>0
, we have

(1) The following regularity estimates:

\displaystyle\|\Lambda_{\kappa}f\|_{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{s},~~\forall s\geq 0;

(2.5)

\displaystyle|\Lambda_{\kappa}f|_{s}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{s},~~\forall s\geq-0.5;

(2.6)

\displaystyle|\overline{\partial}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}f|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa^{-s}|f|_{1-s},~~\forall s\in[0,1];

(2.7)

\displaystyle|f-\Lambda_{\kappa}f|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\kappa}|\overline{\partial}{}f|_{0.5}.

(2.8)

(2) Commutator estimates: Define the commutator
[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]g:=\Lambda_{\kappa}(fg)-f\Lambda_{\kappa}(g)
. Then it satisfies

\displaystyle|[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]g|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{L^{\infty}}|g|_{0},

(2.9)

\displaystyle|[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]\overline{\partial}{}g|_{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}|g|_{0},

(2.10)

\displaystyle|[\Lambda_{\kappa},f]\overline{\partial}{}g|_{0.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}|g|_{0.5}.

(2.11)

Proof.

See
[
17
,
31
,
63
,
92
]
.
∎

2.3
Elliptic estimates

Lemma 2.5

(
Hodge-type decomposition
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth vector field and
s\geq 1
, then it holds that

\|X\|_{s}\lesssim\|X\|_{0}+\|\text{curl }X\|_{s-1}+\|\text{div }X\|_{s-1}+|\overline{\partial}{}X\cdot N|_{s-1.5}.

(2.12)

Proof.

This follows from the well-known identity
-\Delta X=\text{curl }\text{curl }X-\nabla\text{div }X
and integrating by parts.
∎

Lemma 2.6

(
Christodoulou-Lindblad elliptic estimate
)
.

If
f|_{\partial\Omega}=0
, then the following elliptic estimate holds for
r\geq 2
.

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{a}f\|_{r}\leq P(\|\eta\|_{r})\Big(\|\Delta_{a}f\|_{r-1}+\|\overline{\partial}{}\eta\|_{r}\|f\|_{r}\Big).

(2.13)

Proof.

See Ginsberg-Lindblad-Luo
[
28
, Appendix B]
. When
r=1
,
\|\eta\|_{r},\|\overline{\partial}{}\eta\|_{r}
should be replaced by
H^{2}
-norm. The proof of this version can be found in Luo-Zhang
[
63
, Lemma 2.7]
.
∎

3
A priori estimates of the nonlinear approximation system

We define
\tilde{\eta}
to be the smoothed version of
\eta
by

\begin{cases}-\Delta\tilde{\eta}=-\Delta\eta~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\tilde{\eta}=\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma.\end{cases}

(3.1)

Denote
\tilde{a}:=[\partial\tilde{\eta}]^{-1}
to be the cofactor matrix,
\tilde{J}:=\det[\partial\tilde{\eta}]
to be the Jacobian determinant and
\tilde{A}:=\tilde{J}\tilde{a}
. Then we define the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation system of (
1.14
) by

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\eta=v+\psi~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}v=-\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div}_{\tilde{a}}v=-e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}h~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\text{div }({\mathbf{F}^{0}}^{\top})_{j}:=\partial_{k}\mathbf{F}^{0}_{kj}=-e^{\prime}(\mathfrak{h}_{0})(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\mathfrak{h}_{0}~~~&\text{in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{t}|_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{T}([0,T]\times\Gamma)~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
h=0,\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot N=0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
-\frac{\partial h}{\partial N}\geq c_{0}>0~~~&\text{on }\Gamma,\\
(\eta,v,h)|_{t=0}=(\text{Id},v_{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0}).\end{cases}

(3.2)

Here the term
\psi=\psi(\eta,v)
is a correction term which solves the following Laplacian equation

\begin{cases}\Delta\psi=0&~~~\text{in }\Omega,\\
\psi=\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}\sum_{L=1}^{2}\left(\overline{\Delta}{}\eta_{l}\tilde{a}^{Lk}\overline{\partial}{}_{L}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}v-\overline{\Delta}{}\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta_{k}\tilde{a}^{Lk}\overline{\partial}{}_{L}v\right)&~~~\text{on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(3.3)

where
\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}
denotes the standard Littlewood-Paley projection in
\mathbb{T}^{2}
which removes the zero-frequency part.
\overline{\Delta}{}:=\partial_{1}^{2}+\partial_{2}^{2}
denotes the tangential Laplacian operator.

Remark
.

1.

The correction term
\psi\to 0
as
\kappa\to 0
. We introduce it to eliminate the higher order boundary terms which appears in the tangential estimates of
v
. These higher order boundary terms are zero when
\kappa=0
but cannot be controlled when
\kappa>0
.

2.

The Littlewood-Paley projection is necessary here because we will repeatedly use the following inequality, otherwise the zero frequency part cannot be controlled

|\overline{\Delta}{}^{-1}\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}f|_{s}\approx|\mathbb{P}_{\neq 0}f|_{{H}^{s-2}}\approx|f|_{\dot{H}^{s-2}}.

3.

The initial data is the same of origin system (
1.14
) because the compatibility conditions stay unchanged after mollification due to
\tilde{a}(0)=a(0)=I_{3}
(identity matrix).

In this section, we are going to prove the uniform-in-
\kappa
a priori estimates for the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximation system (
3.2
).

For each
\kappa>0
, we define the energy functional to be

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}(T)

\displaystyle:=\left\|\eta\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left\|v\right\|_{4}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left\|h\right\|_{4}^{2}+\left|\tilde{a}^{3i}\overline{\partial}^{2}\overline{\Delta}\Lambda_{\kappa}\eta_{i}\right|_{0}^{2}

(3.4)

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{t}v\right\|_{3}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}\eta\right\|_{3}^{2}+\left\|\partial_{t}h\right\|_{3}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\right\|_{2}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{2}\eta\right\|_{2}^{2}+\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{2}h\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}v\right\|_{1}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{3}\eta\right\|_{1}^{2}+\left\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{3}h\right\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle+\left\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}v\right\|_{0}^{2}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\left\|e^{\prime}(h)(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\partial_{t}^{4}\eta\right\|_{0}^{2}+\left\|(e^{\prime}(h))^{\frac{3}{2}}\partial_{t}^{4}h\right\|_{0}^{2}

Our conclusion is

Proposition 3.1
.

There exists some
T>0
independent of
\kappa
, such that the energy functional
\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}
satisfies

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}(t)\leq P(\|v_{0}\|_{4},\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|\mathfrak{h}_{0}\|_{4}),

(3.5)

provided the following assumptions hold for all
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle(-\partial h\cdot N)(t)\geq c_{0}/2~~~

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{J}(t)-1\|_{3}+\|\text{Id}-\tilde{a}(t)\|_{3}\leq\delta~~~

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

(3.7)

Remark
.

The first a priori assumption (
3.6
) will be justified by verifying
(-\partial h\cdot N)
is a
C_{t,x}^{0,1/4}
function on the boundary, and thus the positivity of Taylor sign condition must propagate for a positive time. The second a priori assumption (
3.7
) can be easily justified once the energy bounds are established by using
\tilde{a}(T)-\text{Id}=\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{t}\tilde{a}=\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{a}:\partial_{t}\partial\tilde{\eta}:\tilde{a}\,dt
and the smallness of
T
.

In Section
4
, we will prove the local well-posedness of (
3.2
) in an
\kappa
-dependent time interval
[0,T_{\kappa}]
. Therefore, the uniform-in-
\kappa
a priori estimate guarantees that the solution
(\eta(\kappa),v(\kappa),h(\kappa))
to (
3.2
) converges to the solution to the original system (
1.14
) in a
\kappa
-independent time interval
[0,T]
as
\kappa\to 0_{+}
, i.e., local existence of the solution to free-boundary compressible elastodynamic system is established. For simplicity, we omit the
\kappa
and only write
(\eta,v,h)
in this manuscript.

Before going to the proof, we present the estimates of the correction term
\psi
.

Lemma 3.2
.

We have the following estimates for
(v,\psi,\eta)
in (
3.2
).

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\eta\|_{4},

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{4}+\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4}),

(3.9)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\psi\|_{4}+\|\partial_{t}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3}),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\|_{3}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}\psi\|_{2}+\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi\|_{1}\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2},\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}),

(3.12)

\displaystyle\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}\psi\|_{1}+\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi\|_{0}\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2},\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1},\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}v\|_{0}).

(3.13)

and

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4})

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}+\|\partial_{t}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4}),

(3.15)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}\tilde{\eta}\|_{3}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3}),

(3.16)

\|\partial_{t}^{3}\tilde{\eta}\|_{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{1}\lesssim P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}),

(3.17)

\displaystyle\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{4}\tilde{\eta}\|_{1}+\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{4}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{0}\lesssim

\displaystyle P(\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4},\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4},\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2},\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1})

(3.18)

\displaystyle\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{5}\tilde{\eta}\|_{0}\lesssim\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{5}\eta\|_{0}\lesssim

\displaystyle P(\|\eta\|_{4},\|v\|_{4},\|\partial_{t}v\|_{3},\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2},\|\sqrt{e^{\prime}(h)}\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1},\|e^{\prime}(h)\partial_{t}^{4}v\|_{0}),

(3.19)

Proof.

The estimates of
\tilde{\eta},\psi
and their derivatives are exactly the same as
[
92
, Lemma 3.2]
, which can be proved by using the properties of mollifier and elliptic estimates, so we omit the details here. We just show the estimates of
\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi\|_{4}
and
\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}
.

We recall the definition of
\tilde{\eta}
in (
3.1
) and take
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)
in that equation to get

\begin{cases}-\Delta((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta})=-\Delta((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)-[(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial),\Delta]\eta+[(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial),\Delta]\tilde{\eta}~~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}=(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta~~~&\text{ on }\Gamma.\end{cases}

(3.20)

By standard elliptic estimates we have

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|-\Delta((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)-[(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial),\Delta]\eta+[(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial),\Delta]\tilde{\eta}\right\|_{2}+\left|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\eta\right|_{7/2}

(3.21)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4}+\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4}\|\eta\|_{4}+\left|\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)\right|_{7/2}+\left|\left[(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial),\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2}\right]\right|_{7/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4}+\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4}\|\eta\|_{4}

\displaystyle+\sum_{L=1}^{2}\left|\left[\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2},\mathbf{F}^{0}_{Lj}\right]\overline{\partial}{}_{L}\eta\right|_{1/2}+\left|\left[\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2},\mathbf{F}^{0}_{Lj}\right]\overline{\partial}{}_{L}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\eta\right|_{1/2}+\left|\left[\overline{\partial}{}^{3},[\Lambda_{\kappa}^{2},\mathbf{F}^{0}_{Lj}]\overline{\partial}{}_{L}\right]\eta\right|_{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4}+\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{4}\|\eta\|_{4}.

The time derivative of
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}
directly follows from
\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{4}
and the estimates of
\|\partial_{t}^{r}\tilde{\eta}\|_{5-r}
. For example,
\|\partial_{t}^{r}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}\|_{4-r}\lesssim\|\mathbf{F}^{0}\|_{3}\|\partial_{t}^{r}\tilde{\eta}\|_{5-r}
for
1\leq r\leq 4
.

The estimates of
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\psi
follows in the same way as
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\tilde{\eta}
, i.e., taking
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)
in the elliptic system (
3.3
) and using standard elliptic estimates, so we omit the proof. Its time derivative can also be controlled by using the bounds of
\partial_{t}^{r}\psi
.
∎

Now we start to analyze the energy
\mathbb{E}_{\kappa}(T)
. The first step is to control the full spatial derivative of
v
and
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
.

3.1
Div-Curl estimates of full spatial derivatives

Let
s=4
and
X=v
and
(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta
in Lemma
2.5
respectively, we have

\displaystyle\|v\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|v\|_{0}+\|\text{div }v\|_{3}+\|\text{curl }v\|_{3}+|\overline{\partial}{}v\cdot N|_{5/2},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta\|_{0}+\|\text{div }((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)\|_{3}+\|\text{curl }((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)\|_{3}+|\overline{\partial}{}((\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)\eta)\cdot N|_{5/2}.

(3.23)

L^{2}
-estimates: Energy conservation

The proof is nearly the same as in (
1.5
). Taking
L^{2}
inner product of the second equation in (
3.2
) and
v
yields

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega}|v|^{2}=-\int_{\Omega}v\cdot(\nabla_{\tilde{a}}h)+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\mathbf{F}^{0}_{j}\cdot\partial)^{2}\eta\cdot v\,dy.

Integrating by parts gives
